# Unfilter digest 12 — pregnancy, birth and the first weeks

Read on 2026-11-17. Share: voice/reddit-ur14.jsonl (401 quote records), voice/reddit-ur15.jsonl (430), voice/reddit-ur27.jsonl (338): **1,169 quotes**. Read the niche README, the current findings and its revised conclusions, every quote field in all three files, and each ledger’s “What these posts add” section. No network or git was used. This is research for the final synthesis, not copy.

Every number below counts quote records or citations, never people. These records were chosen because their source material had previously been excluded; they cannot estimate prevalence. Multiple excerpts and crossposts may describe one voice. The ur27 ledger identifies the paired beyondthebump/pregnant texts behind [pbu27-0116], [pbu27-0117] and [pbu27-0318] as one voice. Retired identifier gaps were preserved. Later-postpartum accounts are relevant where they explicitly discuss pregnancy, birth or early recovery; their later experiences are not automatically first-two-weeks events.

Record quotations are complete quote fields, with their original spelling, punctuation, markup and line breaks, formatted as blockquotes. The separate “Current conclusion” quotations come from synthesis/findings.md. Interpretations, proposed changes and register descriptions are mine. Clinical beliefs, risk figures, allegations, policies and legal expectations remain attributed testimony; no external facts were verified.

## The changes the final synthesis most needs

The clearest direct contradiction is the surviving claim that second-timers differ “not in knowledge.” A subsequent pregnancy may be a first delivery after loss, or a first spontaneous labor, induction or NICU experience. Pregnancy count, deliveries, surviving children and procedures experienced need separate treatment.

The stage arc needs a decision before preparation and a loss pathway after it. Parents discuss whether to continue, attachment, termination, miscarriage procedures, remembrance, and postpartum recovery without a surviving baby. Supportive relationships or money do not settle the decision or its emotional aftermath.

The partner account must name coercion, violence, sexual pressure and control of care or postpartum arrangements alongside household work. Practical help and emotional support can diverge. Partners also describe grief, exhaustion, changed attachment and uncertainty about their own place.

Care navigation includes unresolved results, contradictory explanations, diagnostic versus screening choices, repeated treatment failure, and deliberately withholding information because disclosure feels dangerous. Some clinicians listen and help. An additional scan, referral or positive story is not a universal answer.

Feeding and recovery need actual procedures and contingencies: letdown panic, formula preparation, flow and volume conflicts, physical supply limits, tissue separation, readmission and repair surgery. A supplies checklist or two-week endpoint misses much of what these quotes say.

The money register adds willingness to pay for guided postpartum fitness and many purchases or care decisions, alongside inability to afford tests, treatment or safe departure. None establishes willingness to buy the proposed Mamivio guide. Requests are concrete but varied; do not turn a request for a clinician, treatment explanation or reassurance into demand for a PDF.

## Patterns and their evidence

### 1. Pregnancy count does not tell us whether someone has delivered, recognised labor, or used the procedures now being offered.

**Belongs in:** §9; §10 For 3 and For 8; §16.5; §17.3.

**Relationship to current findings:** Contradicts the surviving knowledge claim; confirms the restored §9 qualification.

**Current conclusion:** “Second-timers' needs differ in fear and logistics, not in knowledge (17.4).”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Replace this sentence. Ask separately about pregnancy count, losses, living children, previous deliveries, gestation reached, procedures experienced and time since the last birth. §16.5 also says “Second-time content must assume she has a child to mind”: remove that assumption. A second pregnancy can be a first hoped-for delivery. Even five pregnancies do not establish experience of going overdue. Previous birth can teach fear without teaching a new induction, NICU stay or delivery method.

### 2. After loss, milestones and purchases can feel like risking another public and private bereavement.

**Belongs in:** §1; §2; §6 Waiting/reassurance; §9; §10 personalisation.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Another pregnancy means another body, history and child to care for”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Develop pregnancy after loss as a situation with its own preparation barriers. Reaching twelve weeks, reassuring screening or already having a living child need not make shopping, disclosure or imagining a baby possible. Replace a universal announcement/buying calendar with choices about privacy, hope and readiness; include the experience of retracting an announcement. Preserve positive subsequent pregnancies alongside fear. The heading's reference to a child must not imply that every previous pregnancy produced a surviving child.

### 3. Parents need to understand what tests establish, what remains uncertain, and what decision or appointment follows.

**Belongs in:** §4 Consent/explanations; §5 Being believed; §6; §17.4 care navigation.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The issue is more specific than plans changing: some women describe being denied information, not asked, shamed, or unable to obtain a date or referral; others describe respectful discussion and an easy booking.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Include ambiguity before a confirmed birth pathway: sac/heartbeat/dating discrepancies, growth percentiles disclosed in portals, carrier results, uncertain viability, screening versus diagnosis, invasive-test tradeoffs, and different specialists' explanations. A list of tests is insufficient without explaining the question each test answers, the wait and whom to ask. The clinical interpretations, numerical risks and recommendations below remain the speakers' accounts, including contradictory or mistaken ones.

**Verbatim evidence:**

**[pbu14-0005]**

Has anyone ever dealt with these issues at the anatomy scan? Is amniocentesis worth it with a negative NIPT? Has anyone done amnio and how painful and scary is it?

I was under the impression that he was in the less than 10% but am now totally freaking out that he is in the less than 1% which signals that something could be significantly wrong. My blood pressure is normal, my amniotic fluid is normal, and his heart rate were all normal, but I can’t help but feel like I should have been sent straight to the hospital for further testing instead of just sent home to wait for MFM to call and get me in for more testing.

I’m so scared that my baby has this condition, but a diagnosis can only be confirmed if I get a an amniocentesis. I have an appointment in two weeks but it feels like a lifetime.

> A few days ago I went for a paid ultrasound to get a more thorough check-up (I’m a huge overthinker and worrier) and to get 3D pictures of my son. Everything seemed fine at first — the doctor was very talkative and joking around until he suddenly went completely silent when examining the heart. The silence felt so long. Eventually I gathered the courage to ask if everything was okay, but he just ignored the question. I started getting nervous and asked again, and he replied that my midwife would discuss the results with me. I was slowly starting to have a panic attack and asked if something was wrong. I don’t remember exactly how he explained it, but this is what appeared in my medical records:  
> “There is right heart dominance in the fetal heart along with mild tricuspid regurgitation. No signs of Ebstein’s anomaly. The pulmonary veins drain into the left atrium. The ratio between the aortic isthmus and ductus arteriosus is reassuring. No clear hypoplasia of the aortic arch or significant right ventricular outflow obstruction is observed. A repeat fetal echocardiographic evaluation is recommended in 2 weeks to assess progression.”  
> The ultrasound specialist explained that this kind of finding can appear in the third trimester and that I shouldn’t worry — it may stabilize or disappear completely. But that long silence still doesn’t give me peace of mind, especially because none of my previous ultrasounds showed any abnormalities. Has anyone experienced something similar? What was the outcome? ### 4. Checking can briefly soothe, create a new threat, or make normal improvement itself frightening; some parents explicitly want honest uncertainty.

**Belongs in:** §6 Waiting/reassurance; §4 Google/AI; §10 Against 2; §16.1.

**Relationship to current findings:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “More information can reassure or renew fear.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Name the mechanisms: inspecting toilet paper, repeated test-line comparison, temperature tracking, symptom monitoring, Doppler use, risk calculators, alarming story feeds and the clinician's tone. Do not infer that another scan or positive story will settle fear, or that all questions are irrational: parents also ask for negative outcomes and specificity without false hope. The quoted beliefs about symptoms, devices, statistics and exposure are testimony, not verified risk estimates.

**Verbatim evidence:**

### 5. Wanting children, wanting this pregnancy now, continuing it and feeling attached are different decisions and experiences.

**Belongs in:** §1; §2 stage arc; §6; §9; a new pregnancy-options subsection.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends the portrait and contradicts an assumed settled path to birth.

**Current conclusion:** “She is pregnant, often for the first time”

**What should change / what this confirms:** The reader portrait and stage arc must include deciding whether to continue, including a planned pregnancy, prior infertility, supportive relationships and adequate money. Neither gratitude nor resources settle readiness. Include relief without regret, certainty with grief, regret after termination, and uncertainty after a scan; do not impose a single emotional ending. The useful pregnancy-options workbook is an observed resource, not evidence that the proposed guide has a buyer.

**Verbatim evidence:**

Or am I waiting for something to replace my actual problems? Something to project on? Something to use to make me feel better or proud or meaningful?

But still, if we kept it he would be more than okay with that path too, he would still support, he would stay in both of our lives okay & do what a man needs to do, make the relationship work for the baby. This is such a difficult situation to be in & I know millions of women have been here, I know I need to follow my heart and do what is right. What if I go through with an abortion and regret it everyday till my next pregnancy. What if I have the baby, and can’t give it the best more beautiful life it deserves. ### 6. Pregnancy decisions and postpartum arrangements can be controlled by partners or families rather than shared with the pregnant parent.

**Belongs in:** §5 partners/relatives; §6; §9; §10 For 6; §12 and §17.7 gaps.

**Relationship to current findings:** Contradicts the main mental-load framing as a sufficient account; extends restored pressure evidence.

**Current conclusion:** “From her side, the gap is mental load more than absence:”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Qualify this surviving sentence explicitly. Separate lack of knowledge or initiative from threats, financial dependency, pressure to continue or terminate, interference with contraception or sterilisation, and decisions made behind the parent's back. A short task list cannot explain or resolve coercion. An author's pregnancy-entrapment suspicion remains a suspicion. Family, religious and racial pressure can operate in either direction.

**Further supporting IDs:** [pbu14-0181] [pbu14-0261] [pbu14-0347] [pbu15-0263] [pbu15-0364] [pbu15-0382] [pbu27-0149]

### 7. Some parents are planning pregnancy and birth while facing assault, choking, sexual coercion, racism or threats to their lives.

**Belongs in:** §5; §6; §9; §12 selection gaps; new safety/privacy circumstances.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends the acknowledged exclusion gap into direct evidence.

**Current conclusion:** “Supportive accounts coexist with minimisation, absence, pressure and unequal work.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** The restored range needs explicit violence and sexual coercion, including accounts from partners subjected to aggression. Do not describe danger as a communication mismatch or solve it with shared chores. Privacy, who can know a location or pregnancy, hospital access and available support can be central. Record allegations and perceived danger as testimony; do not turn the quoted fears into verified legal or clinical claims.

**Further supporting IDs:** [pbu14-0218]

### 8. Wanted pregnancy can coexist with depression, rage, suicidal thoughts, fear of death and a sense of losing oneself.

**Belongs in:** §2; §6 Fear/shame/identity; §9; §10 limits of preparation.

**Relationship to current findings:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The restored words include panic, sadness, lack of excitement, body distress, regret and fear of not bonding or surviving birth.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Keep the intensity and the authors' distinctions: passive wishes, frightening intrusive thoughts, explicit suicidal thoughts, dreams, self-harm and denials of intent are different descriptions. Wanting a baby or having survived infertility does not make depression impossible. Shame about gratitude can add another burden. A preparation guide cannot be treated as treatment or promised protection from these experiences.

### 9. Asking for mental-health help can lead to treatment tradeoffs, failed referrals, waiting rules or repeated options that have already failed.

**Belongs in:** §5 Being believed; §6; §8 cost of care; §10 care navigation.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Care navigation includes failed referrals and follow-up, dismissive or blaming treatment, unexplained results and conflicting professionals.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Add treatment history, access timing, functioning without ADHD treatment, medication effects, fear of destabilisation before birth, and the cost of continuing therapy. 'Seek support' is insufficient when the author already has therapists, psychiatrists and an OB. Preserve claims about offered medicines and their safety as reported care, without endorsing or comparing treatments.

### 10. Fear of judgement, compulsory involvement or losing a baby can make people hide symptoms, histories or abuse and postpone care.

**Belongs in:** §5 care trust; §6; §9; new disclosure/access subsection.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The earlier contacts-and-checklists account understated loss of trust and the reluctance to ask again.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Distinguish not being heard from choosing not to disclose, refusing an offered scan or waiting for a test to become negative. Document the stated reasons: perceived child-protection or drug-testing consequences, old self-harm scars, provider judgement, unwanted appointments, and fear of a diagnosis. These are perceptions and reported choices, not established laws, testing policies or consequences.

### 11. Symptoms and attempts at relief form interacting tradeoffs that can make ordinary eating, sleeping, moving and caregiving impossible.

**Belongs in:** §2 Symptoms/relief/functioning; §4 products/remedies; §6; §8.

**Relationship to current findings:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The broken week-by-week script includes persistent pain, exhaustion, vomiting and incontinence, repeated remedies and disrupted sleep.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Show the sequence rather than a remedy list: treating vomiting without relieving nausea, sedation versus sickness, water or food versus reflux, pain versus medication guilt, and recurring itch despite treatment. Include chronic illness and symptoms arriving earlier than expected. Being told that the baby is fine can leave the parent's own incapacity unanswered. All treatment effects are attributed reports.

### 12. Food, exercise and exposure rules can create guilt while disability, pain, trauma and resources limit what a parent can do.

**Belongs in:** §2; §6 Identity/body; §7 advice; §9 personalisation.

**Relationship to current findings:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Exercise appears as identity, a hoped-for preparation method, a source of guilt and a question about pain or safety.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Include the inability to follow a recommendation, not just knowledge of it: fainting limits accompanied walks; early pain stops a workout; yoga discloses a private pregnancy; food fears remove remaining tolerable meals. Substance accounts include cessation, craving, dependence, inadvertent exposure, medication substitutions and loss of shared rituals. Quoted exposure fears and cessation beliefs are evidence about decision-making, not clinical guidance.

**Verbatim evidence:**

### 13. Pain relief, procedures and consent differ in both directions; flexibility does not account for unavailable relief, coercion or respectful emergency care.

**Belongs in:** §4 Birth preferences and Consent; §6 birth fear; §10 Against 6.

**Relationship to current findings:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “A flexible plan helps some people, but the earlier flexibility conclusion cannot explain every outcome or place responsibility for traumatic care on the woman.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Retain full clinical context and what the parent says about treatment: ineffective operative anaesthesia, low platelets preventing an epidural, stitching before lidocaine works, uncertain consent to a sweep, and access to alternatives. Put positive ECV, induction, side/back positioning, informed emergency care and smooth caesarean accounts alongside trauma. Parents also reject moral hierarchies around epidurals. Do not infer the clinical appropriateness of a recommendation from the author's reaction.

**Further supporting IDs:** [pbu14-0121] [pbu14-0174] [pbu15-0043] [pbu27-0006] [pbu27-0007] [pbu27-0071] [pbu27-0159]

### 14. Birth or hospital admission can arrive before classes, equipment, childcare and the assumed preparation window.

**Belongs in:** §2 third-trimester arc; §4 classes; §10 planning; §17.2.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Third trimester: time runs out, then won't end.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Include preterm birth, immediate delivery decisions and prolonged antenatal admission. A dated plan needs earlier contingencies and acknowledgement that the planned preparation may never happen. Recognising labor can be difficult without out-of-hours advice; anticipated analgesia may be unavailable on arrival. Include the limit of a schedule when admission, transfer or a sudden clinical decision controls events.

### 15. Recovery can improve and then deteriorate, need investigation or repair, and make basic self-care or baby care unavailable beyond two weeks.

**Belongs in:** §2 Recovery/feeding/sleep; §4; §5 care; §10 For 5.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Make the first two weeks a full section: feeding, sleep, recovery supplies, the partner's shifts, visitors.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Keep the first weeks central but add follow-up and re-entry after readmission, unresolved bleeding, retained tissue, continence problems and wound repair. The birth report may reveal injuries that were never explained. Later records are relevant where they describe birth or early recovery; do not label the entire later experience a first-two-weeks event. Supplies and a nominal six-week clearance do not describe the whole trajectory.

### 16. Feeding needs specific instruction and coordination, and can involve panic, painful procedures or physical supply limits rather than lack of effort.

**Belongs in:** §2 Recovery/feeding; §6 explicit asks; §10 For 5.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The first weeks include physical incapacity, feeding pressure, conflicting instructions, loneliness, regret and delayed connection.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Make the feeding gap concrete: letdown panic, dread at four days, formula preparation at night and outside the home, nipple flow, intake targets, jaundice/dehydration, pumping, cost of lactation follow-up and interruptions for surgery. Avoid assuming breastfeeding is merely difficult but achievable with persistence. Agreement to supplement is not necessarily agreement to abandon breastfeeding. These are reported care instructions, not endorsed feeding protocols.

**Evidence also used here, reproduced in earlier patterns:** [pbu15-0125] [pbu15-0142] [pbu27-0107]

**Further supporting IDs:** none assigned exclusively to this pattern; its evidence is quoted or cross-referenced above.

### 17. Birth and a healthy baby do not guarantee instant love, and an immediate or joyful bond also appears after fear and traumatic birth.

**Belongs in:** §2 first weeks; §6 fear/shame; §7 stock reassurance; §9.

**Relationship to current findings:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “A healthy baby or an outwardly successful birth does not settle what the mother experienced; relief and improvement also appear.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Retain both delayed and immediate connection. Loss, anaesthesia, separation, feeding pain or not being able to provide first holds can shape the author's interpretation of being needed. Preserve accounts that no feeling changed at birth beside the father whose feelings changed at once. Neither can be promised as the reader's future.

### 18. Practical help, emotional care and actually taking a promised shift are different forms of support.

**Belongs in:** §5 partners; §7; §10 For 6 and postpartum support.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends and qualifies.

**Current conclusion:** “Supportive accounts coexist with minimisation, absence, pressure and unequal work.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Specify whether help is available, offered, delivered, usable and emotionally supportive. A partner can do the housework and still blame or humiliate; another quietly shares restrictions and arranges childcare. Being told to ask does not solve a request already refused. A cleaner can be a mismatch for the need for daily shared tasks. Nearby help can become unusable if a baby will not tolerate others.

> I didn’t think pregnancy would affect me this much, i was sick for weeks 6-12. I could barely eat and all i did was sleep. Once I hit 15-17 weeks i could finally good again and felt more normal. I even had somewhat of a libido again! Once i got to mid to late 20 weeks is when everything felt like it was gone again. For me sex is a very intimate and one of the ways I can feel connected to my partner and I can say without a doubt I have zero libido. I feel so disconnected from my husband for that reason, it’s like i lost all feeling down there. I don’t even find myself having any thoughts of it. I also feel huge now ( 34 going on 35 weeks) and it’s made me very very insecure about my body as i wasnt very skinny before being pregnant anyways so add on another almost 50 lbs and I feel like a beached whale who ran a marathon trying to do any position. Needless to say I used to be the one who would initiate most nights to now not even thinking about it anymore. 
> 
> As much as I am grateful I could get pregnant and i’ve been able to carry a healthy baby this far I can’t help but just lay here and miss my old body and my old marriage.

### 19. Partners need recognition as grieving, frightened and sometimes exhausted parents, including when the relationship ends.

**Belongs in:** §5 other side; §9 partners; §10 partner format.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The gap includes practical support during pain, illness and recovery and partners’ own fear and exhaustion.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Give the partner perspective its own words about grief, belonging, attachment, burnout and coparenting rather than treating a partner only as a helper. A non-birthing parent may need care or be subjected to violence. Preserve supportive fathers and their changing attachment; do not equate a self-description with the other parent's account or treat crossposts as independent people.

**Further supporting IDs:** none assigned exclusively to this pattern; its evidence is quoted or cross-referenced above.

### 20. Visitor and disclosure plans concern bodily privacy, trust, perceived danger and usable help, and the desired boundary can change after birth.

**Belongs in:** §3; §5 relatives; §6 visitors; §7; §10 For 7.

**Relationship to current findings:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The visitor calendar alone misses bodily privacy, trust and the mother’s ability to recover.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Include control over scans and announcements, who is in the birth room, touch, vaccines, illness exposure and pressure to host while bleeding or feeding. Preserve requests to hold the baby and changed no-visitor plans alongside requests for privacy. Keep severe concerns and allegations distinct from routine hosting; a relative's gift does not settle access, and the author may feel guilty despite practical generosity.

> I also don’t intend for her to have an active grandmother role. Even if she stays sober, I don’t want my child exposed to the instability I’ve experienced. I’m worried about boundary issues because she has a history of showing up places unexpectedly, making false accusations, and creating chaos. I don’t want my children becoming attached to someone whose behavior has been so unpredictable, and I don’t want them caught up in the same instability I grew up with.
> 
> I think the hardest part emotionally is that she seems to expect to step into this happy chapter of life without ever acknowledging the damage she caused. She has never apologized or taken responsibility for anything.
> 
> So I guess my core question is:  
> Am I wrong for feeling like I don’t owe her a pregnancy announcement?
> 
> If she eventually asks me directly if I’m pregnant, I’ll answer honestly. But I don’t feel any desire to call her and share the news like I have with the people who have actually been there for me.
> 
> For people who are estranged from a parent, how did you handle pregnancy announcements and becoming a parent yourselves? Did you tell them proactively, or did you let them find out on their own? How did you handle access to their grandchildren?

**[pbu15-0355]**

> Anyways, does anyone have any advice on how to tell my parents that I’m pregnant? My mom is super religious (catholic) and Mexican, she’s very opposed to abortion and divorce. Abortion was a thought for me but I ultimately decided I could not go through with it. My mom would always tell me that before I have kids she would want me to get married. I personally do not want to get married possibly ever? Anyways, after typing this it feels like a lot but I’m just trying to really get some desperately needed advice.

**[pbu15-0371]**

> All of that is annoying, but the real nail in the coffin is with him being born at the end of October, we asked our families to get updated TDAP and flu shots. My family agreed with no argument. They basically got into a huge argument about it and went into why vaccines don’t work, they’re healthy and don’t need it (they’re not, both have diabetes, his dad had a stroke recently, his mom has glaucoma), that the Bible said this (medical things) would be the reason families get torn apart, and they bet that my parent’s agreed. We didn’t expect for it to be an argument or else my husband would’ve been more prepared. We are kind of torn with how to move forward. 
> 
> We have let so many things go when they do this (there is never any compromise, it’s their way or the highway), but we are both feeling like we don’t want to start our family letting yet another thing go with them. They also aren’t super cognizant of when they are sick and being around people so we feel more strongly about having another rule with them than most people. We’d like to say okay then that means you can’t meet him for 2 months. BUT my husband is hesitant because it is a real possibility that his mom would cut us off and stop talking to us. He doesn’t really mind that lol but he doesn’t want his relationship with his dad to suffer. My husband also feels upset about missing the baby’s first holidays with his family. I also feel like I don’t want my husband in that situation because I do want the baby to have a relationship with his grandparents. However, sometimes they do not show us that they really care with the way they act. My husband keeps saying they obviously don’t care about the way they make me feel, so why should I care about the way this makes them feel. He wants to try to have a conversation with them but is worried it would turn into another argument.

### 21. Pregnancy planning includes surviving the current job and protecting access to care, not just calculating leave after delivery.

**Belongs in:** §8 leave/cost of functioning; §9 work/country; §10 For 9; §17.2.

**Relationship to current findings:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “A calendar is useful to some people but cannot solve the capacity, employment or access problem;”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Connect current symptoms, panic, standing work, withheld breaks, disciplinary action, job loss, insurance activation/expiry, income and education. Add escaping abuse at the expense of employment and maternity leave, and the practical impact of shelter curfews. Paid entitlement and actual willingness to take leave are different. Preserve cooperative leave planning. All employer and insurance rules remain the speaker's understanding, not verified entitlements.

### 22. Spending and affordability concern care, relief, information, safety and ordinary equipment; they do not establish demand for this guide.

**Belongs in:** §8 all money; §10 Against 1 and Against 7; §16.3; §17.5.

**Relationship to current findings:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “There is no direct buyer evidence for the proposed guide.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Retain that conclusion. Add explicit willingness to pay for guided postpartum exercise, paid options/testing, purchases prompted by fear and refused or unaffordable services. Money can block a pregnancy test, dental care, therapy, leaving safely or follow-up; financial help can also remove an entire bill. Prices for scans, procedures, a nursery listing, movers or clothing do not price a Mamivio guide. The complete money register below distinguishes purchases, plans, questions, gifts, coverage and quotes.

### 23. Body image, eating-disorder history, nonbinary identity and gender expectations can change the meaning of ordinary pregnancy advice.

**Belongs in:** §6 Identity/body and fear/shame; §9 new segments; §10 personalisation.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The restored words include panic, sadness, lack of excitement, body distress, regret and fear of not bonding or surviving birth.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Add explicitly nonbinary experiences, dysphoria, conditional family support around gendered baby rituals, eating-disorder relapse fears and weight commentary. Preserve gender disappointment in both directions without equating it with an eating disorder or gender identity. Distinguish losing bodily comfort, sexuality, career and personal identity. Keep self-identified nonbinary authors as parents rather than inferring motherhood or diagnoses.

**Verbatim evidence:**

### 24. Preparation also includes miscarriage, termination, bereavement and physical recovery when no surviving newborn comes home.

**Belongs in:** §2 arc/first weeks; §4 procedures; §5; §6; a new loss-care subsection.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends and corrects the assumed endpoint of the stage arc.

**Current conclusion:** “The first two weeks: the part nobody prepared her for.”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Add the pathway to the existing preparation arc: questions about home versus hospital management, tablets versus surgery, pain, tests, burial or cremation, hospital experiences, grief and later recovery. Postpartum care is still needed after loss; a parent's living children or the short duration of pregnancy do not remove grief. Do not assume every termination brings regret, or that choosing it removes the right to mourn.

I got an appointment for a scan and went thinking I would be having a miscarriage as I had no symptoms of anything else other than the bleeding. 
> 
> Turns out I had an ectopic pregnancy and the tube was already leaking blood into my pelvis. I needed emergency surgery to have the tube removed. ### 25. Parents also plan around pets, grief, housing, school, absent parents, paternity uncertainty and social belonging.

**Belongs in:** §1; §5; §6; §9; material for What fits nowhere.

**Relationship to current findings:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Name her week **and** her pregnancy number **and** her situation (working or not, US or UK, older child or not, partner's leave), and say what to do now and what can wait”

**What should change / what this confirms:** Expand situation-based personalisation beyond the existing trimester/pregnancy-number/country/work checklist. Include current educational needs, late recognition of pregnancy, housing and shelter conditions, pet adjustment and bereavement, paternity uncertainty, lack of family models and friends who withdraw or compete. These records do not establish a separate product market. A few very short excerpts are retained as supporting IDs with their source context rather than inflated into an independent pattern.

**Verbatim evidence:**

## Outliers and counter-examples

These accounts cut against blanket conclusions, including inevitable distress, absent support, failed preparation, regretted termination, difficult surgery, or scans that only worsen anxiety. They do not cancel the contrary accounts or establish probabilities.

**Counter-example:** Deliberately choosing a positive attitude after losses; fear is not universal or necessarily the only stance.

**[pbu14-0040]**

> I know we aren’t out of the woods yet, and realistically something can happen at any time, but I am determined to keep a positive attitude about this pregnancy, and try not to worry unless my healthcare team tells me I have a reason to.

**Counter-example:** Joy and excitement about sharing pregnancy with a sister.

**[pbu14-0102]**

> I'm so ridiculously happy and over the moon for her. She's a fantastic sister and I want nothing but happiness every day for her. I'm so excited to be sharing this journey with her!

**Counter-example:** Joy and teamwork at one week postpartum despite a traumatic operative birth.

**[pbu14-0117]**

> my husband was and has been an absolute godsend. i can confidently say that things are going so well mostly due to his love and support. even despite how horrible my birth experience was, now i'm a week pp and i'd go through it again for this. i love taking night shifts with him, i love his little face, i love his chubby cheeks. i love watching the love of my life become a parent and being a team. i love caring for our child together. obviously i still get stressed and things are really hard sometimes, especially with the sleep deprivation and emotional shifts, but i've never been this happy in my life despite that.

**Counter-example:** ECV described as unexpectedly quick and painless; keep the after-effects in the companion account too.

**[pbu14-0167]**

> What? It was so fast the anaesthesia hadn’t even reach the maximum yet and I just didn’t have time to process what happened. Looks like baby let them move him freely without restriction. Absolutely no pain or discomfort, I couldn’t feel anything, not even pressure, because it was so fast I just didn’t have tine to process it.

**Counter-example:** The same ECV account distinguishes the painless procedure from difficult recovery-room shaking and low blood pressure.

**[pbu14-0168]**

> The worst part was after that as I had to stay in the reanimation room for 2 hours until the spinal started to disappear, and because my blood pressure was very low due to the procedure. Despite the ritodrina didn’t give me palpitations, what I was during that time was shaky as fuck, like the whole bed was shaking, that was for me the worst part, until the shaky shaky went away. They stabilised BP eith several shots of fenilefrina. Baby was monitored during my whole time in reanimation and he was perfectly fine after the procedure.

**Counter-example:** Seeing the scan changes uncertainty to joy, whereas an unrequested display elsewhere complicates the decision.

**[pbu14-0203]**

> but during the ultrasound, everything changed for me. seeing the baby and hearing the heartbeat made my entire world stop, and in a good way. i felt overwhelmed with emotion: happiness, fear, disbelief, but mostly joy. i haven’t been able to stop thinking about it since. and i think maybe my boyfriend felt it too; he was smiling the entire time (according to him).

**Counter-example:** The birth-plan preference for hands-and-knees did not work; pushing on the back did.

**[pbu14-0364]**

> My hospital had a policy that you can push for an hour with just the midwife and your birth partner before they call in a doctor to evaluate. Pushing went so well at the beginning, I thought it would be over quickly. We tried different positions to figure out which would work best for my baby and for me. I had hands and knees in my birth plan as a preference and it turns out that we both hated that. Side lying worked well enough but pushing on my back ended up being best for both of us.

**Counter-example:** An alarming assisted birth with calm explanations and consent; emergency care is not inherently described as traumatic treatment.

**[pbu14-0365]**

> Suddenly, instead of the quiet room with only the midwife, my husband, and myself, I had an entire medical team. My husband described it as the scariest moment of his life because he wasn't sure either of us were going to make it. The reality is that, if we didn't have access to the care we had, that very well could have been the case. Despite this, the hospital staff remained so calm. They explained things very well, got consent for everything, and acted quickly.

**Counter-example:** Healthy birth after early exposure worry is the author's outcome, not proof that any exposure is safe.

**[pbu14-0371]**

> He came trough a C-section because his heart rate started to drop when I had more intense contractions. The limbical cord was wrapped around him so that was the reason. But all went smoothly and the tears of joy and relief I heard his first cry and saw his little body and face, just the emotions…wild 😁❤️ So yeah now we continue our healthy lives and I hope this post also gives hope to someone who might be similar situation. Thank you all for sharing your thoughts and experiences! 🌞

**Counter-example:** A supportive partner follows the parent's lead while sharing uncertainty.

**[pbu14-0386]**

> My husband is being very supportive. He is basically just following my lead and doing whatever I ask. I think he's excited too, but also afraid to be.

**Counter-example:** Shared restrictions and childcare support without seeking praise.

**[pbu15-0045]**

> During pregnancy, he hasn’t drank or done drugs once (we do some psychedelics from time to time, edibles some nights a week to sleep, etc.) or had sushi. He eats healthy and anytime he asks me to eat healthier it was only food he ate too (damn food aversions had me like a 5 y/o with my diet at times). 
> 
> He works out and supports me getting my exercises by being with our son or paying for a sitter. 
> 
> All of this and most importantly? *He doesn’t say one word or call attention to any of it.*
> 
> He doesn’t expect gratitude or thank yous. He just lives because we’re in it together.

**Counter-example:** Planned caesarean recovery described as a breeze and willingness to pay for guided fitness.

**[pbu15-0089]**

> Had a planned c section and recovery was a breeze. I am planning to start strength training, rebuilding my core and pelvic area. Overwhelmed by tons of resources, please shoot some recommendations on the guided exercise paths or any fitness programs ( I don’t mind paying a little to get fit )

**Counter-example:** Financial assistance covers the whole hospital bill.

**[pbu15-0147]**

> She asked me if I wanted to see if I qualified for financial assistance. She told me it was possible that I could get 60 to 100% of the medical bill covered. With my luck I didn’t think I was going to receive any kind of help. The next thing she told me on the phone you’re covered. We had been joking around, so I thought maybe she was playing some sort of evil prank, so when I asked her to clarify, she said your hospital bill is 100% covered and you don’t owe us anything. I cried right then in there and I’ve pretty much been crying all day. This was a huge burden, lifted off my shoulders and I feel so grateful.

**Counter-example:** Resources are available and the author reports no regret after termination.

**[pbu15-0158]**

> We have a nanny, a person who meal preps for our children, we have really whatever we need or want. In theory, having a third child would have been “easy” given the resources and privileges we have. We could have hired a night nanny until the third one would be 1. My youngest also sleeps well and isn’t a difficult baby by any means. I’m adding all this context not to brag but just to say, we have all of this and I feel no guilt about aborting this pregnancy. I thought I might but I never did. I had it done, I am bleeding but I feel indifferent about it. I’m not sure if these feelings are normal and maybe one day I’ll share this with my husband but not in the foreseeable.

**Counter-example:** A father's bond changes immediately at birth; this cannot override delayed-bond accounts.

**[pbu15-0161]**

> I'm telling you that it was like a switch just flipped when he was born and suddenly all of these feelings I didn't have at all, that I had been waiting to hit me, all hit me at once. All of the feelings my girlfriend has had toward our baby from the beginning, even after we found out about the diagnosis, things she's been talking about feeling for months and the whole reason she couldn't get an abortion even though the idea of the two of us having a child with a disability right now seemed like the worst idea I could think of, all made sense. A few people told me this would happen but I didn't believe them. They kept reassuring me when he was born I'd feel differently. I figured I'd love my own kid, but I just didn't think I'd feel it instantly since no matter what I did before he was born, I just can't say I felt any sort of genuine feelings at all.

**Counter-example:** Hospital miscarriage management is experienced as contained and supported.

**[pbu15-0303]**

> But to be honest, im very grateful now. It feels like everything that happend that day, stayed in the hospital. I didnt need to suffer in my shower, me and my husband just had each outher in the hopsital for 10 hours and i got all his support and attention i needed that day.

**Counter-example:** Hope about a new pregnancy and acceptance that tracking offers limited control.

**[pbu15-0304]**

> I am not sure how this pregnency will go, but i am super excited and have a good feeling about it. We will see, i hope i am right :)
> 
> Why i wanted to write all of this i that sometimes we can not trust the test. We can try to figure out everything our body is doing,but in the end of the day we have so little control of this.

**Counter-example:** No regret about pregnancy and confidence about parenting alone despite relationship grief.

**[pbu15-0343]**

> I have zero regrets about getting pregnant, I’ve wanted a baby for years and am at an age where time is of the essence. I know I’ll be absolutely fine on my own. The question is more emotionally driven - a break up is always sad.

**Counter-example:** Savings and nearby parents make extended time off feasible even alone.

**[pbu15-0344]**

> I have enough saved to do at least 9 months off work, even alone. So yes, I think you’re absolutely right about just getting into that mindset now. 
> 
> Though I won’t truly be alone - I have incredibly supportive parents just down the road

**Counter-example:** Instant bond, pumping becoming easier and life described as amazing.

**[pbu27-0073]**

> I bonded instantly to my baby. No real baby blues but some emotions were more intense. Baby is a painful biter and I sentenced to a life of pumping. Not ideal and I struggled with it but my baby is the love of my life and I’d do anything for her. Pumping got easier. Life is amazing.

**Counter-example:** Second postpartum and breastfeeding described as physically and emotionally good.

**[pbu27-0114]**

> I'm now postpartum with my second and breastfeeding has been going great. I've felt amazing this entire postpartum period both physically and emotionally.

**Counter-example:** A smooth birth follows severe anticipatory anxiety; tea and affirmations are the author's attributed preparation, not an efficacy finding.

**[pbu27-0143]**

> it was so smooth. I honestly drank the red raspberry leaf tea 4x a day (mixed with OJ and cran) and practiced affirmations. 8 hour labor start to finish, 30 mins he was out no tears. went home next AM. you got this. you will know when it is time, and truly, i mean this, your body just takes over. i didn’t have any time to rly be anxious

**Counter-example:** Early parenting is easier than expected and not a phase the parent wants to rush through.

**[pbu27-0153]**

> This time is not as hard as I thought it would be. Yes she cries and its a little frustrating sometimes not knowing how to help her but we work through it. Im not wishing this time away, this is the littlest she'll ever be and I want to enjoy every moment.

**Counter-example:** A clinician listens to fears and readily arranges ultrasound.

**[pbu27-0169]**

> I had my first appointment the other day and it was only just suppose to be the pregnancy confirmation appointment but after voicing my fears my doc readily scheduled an ultrasound.

**Counter-example:** Wants all outcomes rather than only hopeful stories and says attachment is limited.

**[pbu27-0245]**

> I just hate being stuck in limbo, not knowing what’s going on! 
> 
> Can anyone share their similar experiences? All experiences are welcome, this was very unplanned so I’m not overly attached to the pregnancy.

**Counter-example:** A couple repairs an argument after acknowledging overwhelm and the need for care.

**[pbu27-0256]**

> We had a good moment together. So I'm feeling better about it, he basically told me that he needed to get over the skin to skin thing, he's had plenty now that we are back in postpartum, but he knew it was a bit ridiculous, because baby and I needed care. He felt really bad, becoming a dad for the first time is overwhelming too. He was struggling with a lot and for whatever reason he said, that was the key issue his brain held onto

**Counter-example:** Pregnancy prompts gratitude and affection toward a partner.

**[pbu27-0259]**

> I’m laying here in bed tonight, with pregnancy insomnia, and I look over at my husband sleeping. The way he is lying, the look on his face, and even the sound of his breathing caught me so off guard because he reminds me so much of my son.   
> I realized there are so many times a day where my son reminds me so much of my husband and often it’s all the things I love about him, his personality, his sense of humor, his smile, and even the way he sleeps. I am so incredibly blessed to be able to see the embodiment of all the reasons I fell in love with my husband living through my son and realizing we did this, together, just the two of us.

**Counter-example:** Sleep improves without medication even though it remains interrupted.

**[pbu27-0305]**

> Small update.  I still am waking up about 2 ish hours after falling asleep (non medicated) however, I am actually able to fall back asleep after about an hour-two hours.  Still not ideal but as least I’m not living on just 2 hours of sleep period!!

**Counter-example:** A living baby and rediscovered hope after loss.

**[pbu27-0316]**

> I learned that pregnancy after loss isn’t just about growing a baby. It’s about learning to believe in happiness again, one day at a time.  
> And then, on September 4, 2026, exactly one year after the day I first found out I was pregnant, I held my baby in my arms. ❤️

**Counter-example:** A partner is kind and involved, while the parent still does not want the relationship.

**[pbu27-0326]**

> fast forward to now, I am 11 weeks and some change pregnant and realising I really do not think I want to be with the father. it is nothing bad he has done or anything at all on him. it’s just me. 
> 
> he is a great guy, incredibly kind, respectful, caring, has been here for me every single step of the way, literally one of the best persons I have dated. and i know he will be an amazing father.

## What fits nowhere

These topics can be cross-referenced to the broad existing sections, but the current findings do not give them a developed section or a usable place in the preparation arc. They should not disappear because the existing outline has no slot.

### A decision pathway before the preparation pathway

There is no developed pregnancy-options section. The stage arc largely assumes an intended birth. Include ambivalence, changing decisions and a resource already used; the workbook's price is not stated.

**[pbu14-0400]**

> *Before I go into things I want to share a resource I found super helpful for people going through pregnancy and decision making. It's a workbook that goes through tons of aspects of pregnancy, parenting, and emotions surrounding all of that.*   
> *Access it at :* [*www.pregnancyoptions.info*](http://www.pregnancyoptions.info/) *– There are two workbooks here: one for helping decide whether keep or abort and another one for post-abortion that offers guidance and such for going through that process.*

**[pbu15-0158]**

> We have a nanny, a person who meal preps for our children, we have really whatever we need or want. In theory, having a third child would have been “easy” given the resources and privileges we have. We could have hired a night nanny until the third one would be 1. My youngest also sleeps well and isn’t a difficult baby by any means. I’m adding all this context not to brag but just to say, we have all of this and I feel no guilt about aborting this pregnancy. I thought I might but I never did. I had it done, I am bleeding but I feel indifferent about it. I’m not sure if these feelings are normal and maybe one day I’ll share this with my husband but not in the foreseeable.

**[pbu27-0093]**

> The strange part is that, although I truly believe this is the right decision, I can't stop feeling sad and guilty. It's like my head knows one thing, but my heart is saying something completely different.

### Loss procedures, remembrance and postpartum without a surviving baby

The delivery/first-weeks sections have no developed sequence for miscarriage management, what happens to the baby, parental mourning or physical recovery without a newborn at home. Include adoption as another account of leaving the ward without a baby, distinct from death.

**[pbu15-0378]**

> 1. What is the process? Do women miscarry at home? Or is there an actual procedure that takes place? How do those two things work if so. 
> 
> 2. Are you able to have any kind of testing done to find out why the baby did not make it? Idk if that would mean genetic testing or any other kind of testing. 
> 
> 3. Are you able to have the baby cremated or buried? Or does the hospital take care of the baby? My mom said the doctor just took the baby away and that was it. My mom said how impersonal it felt and how looking back she wishes it was different. 
> 
> 4. Any other information I should know?

**[pbu15-0077]**

> I mentioned the town we currently live in and a girl commented on the post asking if I was in the local hospital having a baby in Sept 2016. I was, that was when I had my daughter. I confirmed, she reintroduced herself, and we were next to each other on the ward as two teen moms. She said she never saw me in the moms room. I explained that my daughter was adopted and I went straight home.

**[pbu27-0057]**

> Gosh, even months later it’s bothering me and I feel I still do not have closure as I did not ever see her baby. Which is okay, but it was so weird. We were all waiting for a baby and then all of a sudden she was postpartum and no baby.

**[pbu27-0115]**

> After a month everything seems normal  until August 24th at 1:50 Am whe I went to the restroom and it was a gush of blood and a big clot.  My husband took me immediately to the hospital and they had to open my cervix to do a D&C. After the D&C my bleeding got worse so they put a ballon to control the bleeding then took me to MRI to make sure no placenta residue or that I have a term AVM.
> 
> All came back clear, my obgyn believe it might be a very heavy period with and combination of my post partum, still pending on pathology. 
> 
> I did have a blood transfusion and was taken to get a Urtinery Artery Embolization (UAE). Currently am.week out in recovery and 5 more weeks to go.

**[pbu27-0125]**

> It’s been hard. I’m here for my wife and do my best to support her and she does her best to support me. It feels like we only have so long to embrace our grief though when taking care of three other small children. While they can be a lot, they’re certainly beautiful reminders of the family we have. 
> 
> I guess I’m just having a hard time with the grief. It feels like we can’t openly discuss this with people because the pregnancy was so short. I feel guilty for grieving and mourning a child I never knew when others lose their children much later, or in early childhood. 
> 
> I know I shouldn’t look at things that way. I just feel such a mix of emotions.

### Pregnancy after sexual assault under family, racial or religious threat

A country selector and visitor plan do not describe secrecy, inability to attend a clinic, threats to life, racism or a family's pressure about a mixed-race baby.

**[pbu27-0250]**

> I was SAd at a pool party. I don’t know who the father is. I’m now pregnant. Im really panicking because I don’t know what to do. I’m Muslim and Arab so I’m in a very difficult situation. The work/leave calendar has no developed housing or refuge sequence. Safety can cost a job or benefits, and the work needed to leave a shelter can breach its curfew.

The findings have no pet-adjustment or pet-bereavement subsection. These are direct household-planning and emotional needs around pregnancy/newborn life, not generic older-child material.

## Money

This register retains every identified price, purchase, gift purchase, refund/return restriction, cost, financial barrier, financial resource or willingness-to-pay statement, plus explicitly labelled adjacent service bookings and owned supplies where payment is unstated. A quoted price, planned booking, purchase, gift, benefit and paid bill are different evidence. Currency is preserved as stated; dates, drug doses, band sizes and care volumes are not prices. Repeated quotes across this digest remain the same quote records.

**Money entry:** Gift purchase: mother bought baby outfits before the loss; no price.

We were so excited and planning everything out, I told my mom just so I could talk to her about it and she had bought a couple outfits. I know it’s not the end of the world and there will be other chances, but right now I just feel so guilty for some reason

> My husband and I disagree about whether to share the gender of our baby (our first). My thing is that I want a gender neutral nursery and baby items because we hope to have more kids. Money is tight as it is, and it’s going to be tighter once the baby comes and for the next several years after the birth. We don’t have a lot of funds sitting around to buy opposite-gendered items for future kids whose gender is different from this one. We will definitely rely on these gifts for this kid and for any future kids, so if I’m allowed a preference on things like colors (which, as the mom, I feel I am), I’d like gender-neutral gifts. I also don’t want to fret with storing gender-specific items just in case we have another baby of the same gender in the future. However, we both know and agree that his family, specifically, will not listen to or honor any requests we make for gender-neutral baby gifts. My thought is to guarantee that by not sharing the gender.

My youngest also sleeps well and isn’t a difficult baby by any means. I’m adding all this context not to brag but just to say, we have all of this and I feel no guilt about aborting this pregnancy. I thought I might but I never did. I had it done, I am bleeding but I feel indifferent about it. I’m not sure if these feelings are normal and maybe one day I’ll share this with my husband but not in the foreseeable.

**[pbu15-0239]**

> A few days ago I went for a paid ultrasound to get a more thorough check-up (I’m a huge overthinker and worrier) and to get 3D pictures of my son. Everything seemed fine at first — the doctor was very talkative and joking around until he suddenly went completely silent when examining the heart. The silence felt so long. Eventually I gathered the courage to ask if everything was okay, but he just ignored the question. I started getting nervous and asked again, and he replied that my midwife would discuss the results with me. I was slowly starting to have a panic attack and asked if something was wrong. I don’t remember exactly how he explained it, but this is what appeared in my medical records:  
> “There is right heart dominance in the fetal heart along with mild tricuspid regurgitation. No signs of Ebstein’s anomaly. The pulmonary veins drain into the left atrium. The ratio between the aortic isthmus and ductus arteriosus is reassuring. No clear hypoplasia of the aortic arch or significant right ventricular outflow obstruction is observed. A repeat fetal echocardiographic evaluation is recommended in 2 weeks to assess progression.”  
> The ultrasound specialist explained that this kind of finding can appear in the third trimester and that I shouldn’t worry — it may stabilize or disappear completely. But that long silence still doesn’t give me peace of mind, especially because none of my previous ultrasounds showed any abnormalities. Has anyone experienced something similar? What was the outcome? Is it really nothing too serious?

Even though my mind is made up i can’t help but feel a tremendous amount of grief and sorrow.

**Named resources without evidence of a payment:** Oura temperature tracking [pbu14-0272]; existing Nanit use [pbu14-0197]; starting the Whitney Simmons/Alive prenatal programme [pbu14-0284]; the recommended Pregnancy Options workbooks [pbu14-0400]. These establish use, starting or a recommendation, not a purchase or willingness to pay. The source record does not quote a workbook price.

## Requests

The register includes outright requests for goods, tests, clinicians, treatment explanations, instructions, checklists, services or a practical plan. It also explicitly labels requests already made to providers or support people, planned next steps and unmet instruction needs. Those different forms of evidence are identified in each entry. General reassurance, outcome-story and emotional-support requests are not automatically product demand. Requests to support someone else and a clinician’s or partner’s account are retained in their stated roles.

**Request / named next step:** How others plan announcements and grandparent access with an estranged parent.

**[pbu15-0336]**

> I’ve announced my pregnancy publicly on Facebook, so she may already know, but I have intentionally never reached out to tell her.
> 
> Part of me feels guilty because she’s biologically my mother.
> 
> The other part of me feels like she has never actually been my mom. She wasn’t the person who raised me, protected me, or made sacrifices for me. My grandparents did that.
> 
> I also don’t intend for her to have an active grandmother role. Even if she stays sober, I don’t want my child exposed to the instability I’ve experienced. I’m worried about boundary issues because she has a history of showing up places unexpectedly, making false accusations, and creating chaos. I don’t want my children becoming attached to someone whose behavior has been so unpredictable, and I don’t want them caught up in the same instability I grew up with.
> 
> I think the hardest part emotionally is that she seems to expect to step into this happy chapter of life without ever acknowledging the damage she caused. She has never apologized or taken responsibility for anything.
> 
> So I guess my core question is:  
> Am I wrong for feeling like I don’t owe her a pregnancy announcement?
> 
> If she eventually asks me directly if I’m pregnant, I’ll answer honestly. But I don’t feel any desire to call her and share the news like I have with the people who have actually been there for me.
> 
> For people who are estranged from a parent, how did you handle pregnancy announcements and becoming a parent yourselves? Did you tell them proactively, or did you let them find out on their own? How did you handle access to their grandchildren?

She doesn’t like to talk about it so that’s all the info from her I got.

But he doesn’t want to talk about it. So I asked if he wanted to talk about it before or after I lost the baby because I’m preparing myself I don’t want to but I have to listen to what my ob said when my son was born I will always have a chance of PPROM again and that next time me or the child could be unlucky. I asked my “husband” if he was ready for that if he was ready to watch me break because I couldn’t carry another one, asked if he was ready to sit with me while I was told my baby was gone. You’re not getting an abortion, like first off that’s all you got from that subject not the fact that I have a 75% chance of loosing this baby too??? All you can say is I’m not allowed by you to have an abortion if my child is already passed away on YOUR orders? On YOUR behalf???? Sorry that done it for me. **[pbu27-0231]**

> I don't want to panic and go to the emergency room if it's normal. I've tried to change positions and it's still a constant pain though.

**Request / named next step:** What monitoring/treatment for possible newborn withdrawal might involve.

**[pbu27-0244]**

> However, now I’m really stressed/ concerned about my baby having NAS (Narcotic Abstinence Syndrome) at birth and needing to be weaned off the meds (which is something the doctor said they would monitor them for). I feel so guilty that my baby may suffer because of the medication I am on. Are there any other mums who have been in a similar situation that can shed some light on what it might look like should they develop NAS? What was your/ babies expedience like?

**Request / named next step:** Migraine treatment beyond ice and Tylenol.

**[pbu27-0247]**

> What do yall do for a migraine? I am 14 weeks pregnant and this is wild. Ice and Tylenol barely do anything but take the edge off. HELP!

**Request / named next step:** Explicit postpartum supply list, brands/specifications, binders and padsicles.

**[pbu27-0248]**

> **Give me the real deal list of things I will need for postpartum care**. Right now I have a peri bottle and some Depends as well as nipple cream and Dermoplast. 
> 
> Are abdominal binders helpful? Do I need to make those elaborate padsicles I see on social media? Please include any brands or specifications in your suggestions.

**Request / named next step:** Confidential termination route after assault, with fear of family violence and unaffordable pills.

**[pbu27-0251]**

> What’s the safest way I can terminate the pregnancy? Is there something?? There has to be yea😭? Honestly I don’t even care if it’s risky because my father would absolutely send me six feet deep if he finds out.
> 
> going to a clinic is obviously not an option, and getting the pills are way too expensive in an illegal way.

**Request / named next step:** Recovery, precautions, daily activities, warning signs and diet after cerclage were not explained; a detailed unmet instruction need.

**[pbu27-0257]**

> After the cerclage, we were also given very little practical guidance about recovery, precautions, daily activities, warning signs, diet, or how to manage the weeks ahead. We ended up learning most of it ourselves through other doctors and reliable medical resources.

**Request / named next step:** Fatigue relief and iron-testing/treatment guidance.

**[pbu27-0266]**

> Has anyone else experienced this level of tiredness? What else helps? I’m suspecting I could be low on iron, I asked my OB but she said they don’t test for that until they test for GD… not sure why? I’ve been taking iron tablets once every other day and tbh they make me feel a little better but I don’t want to take them every day or up the dose until I know I’m actually iron deficient.

**Request / named next step:** An announcement/privacy plan when family will pressure the parent to terminate.

**[pbu27-0267]**

> All my blood family will tell me to abort it like they did with my son. I personally can't and won't even consider it.
> 
> I want to enjoy my pregnancy and have people be happy for me but my blood family sucks. Do I not say anything till gender reveal which we want to post on social media, till I give birth, or just get it over with and try to deal with the stress, just wait till I see any of them in person(could be years from now) and introduce baby then?

**Request / named next step:** Whether to pay for a private reassurance scan before announcing.

**[pbu27-0268]**

> I’m absolutely terrified to announce my pregnancy because my last scan was at 11 weeks** **instead of 12 weeks. I know people always talk about waiting until 12 weeks, and I’m not sure if getting a reassuring scan at 11 weeks instead of 12 really makes much of a difference. My next appointment isn’t for a little while, so I’m debating whether it’s worth paying for a private ultrasound around 12 weeks just for reassurance before announcing. My biggest fear is a MMC.

**Request / named next step:** Coping help while waiting to access treatment.

**[pbu27-0279]**

> I’m not able to get in yet, so I need help until then. Also, I just really need to know this can and will get better. My OBGYN said it typically only gets worse during pregnant, and I’m terrified of that, as this is already affecting my life so much.

**Request / named next step:** Alternatives after side effects of Reglan/promethazine and repeated medication substitutions.

**[pbu27-0280]**

> Basically I’m curious if anyone else has had these horrible side effects to Reglan or promethazine and what has helped otherwise. I’ve also been told to try vitamin B6 as well as Unisom from the same doctor? I’m desperate and it feels like I’m taking a never ending cycle of medication that stops each side effect

**Request / named next step:** Explicitly tells partner that more than one week of newborn support is needed.

**[pbu27-0287]**

> he wants to come back home the few days leading up to my induction and stay with me the first week. i was like im gonna need you here for more than a week? i dont get a trial run with this baby i don’t understand why its fair for him to do this.

**Request / named next step:** Clear pregnancy-specific yoga poses and exercise guidance; describes unmet instruction rather than a paid-programme request.

**[pbu27-0294]**

> Working out: I do yoga, I actually teach a level 2 class, and I’m still totally unclear about which poses I should and should do. Biking, I want to ride my bike to campus but I’m terrified about getting hit by a giant truck or overheating (I live in a hot state with terrible city planning).

**Request / named next step:** Questions to ask at an appointment about another pregnancy, VBAC and rupture fears.

**[pbu27-0296]**

> But now Im terrified to have more kids, i dont want another csection, It was my worst fear the first time! but im really scared for rupture for VBAC.
> 
> Are there any questions I should ask at this appointment?

**Request / named next step:** Explicit request to have pelvic exams/Pap smears performed by female clinicians after sexual trauma.

**[pbu27-0297]**

> But it gets worse. She asks if I have any sexual trauma. When I answer yes, that I do from my ex husband and I would prefer all Pap smears and pelvic exams to be done by a woman, I’m told that’s simply unlikely because I have to see ALL of the practitioners at their practice.

**Request / named next step:** Explicitly asks to speak to someone about spotting/cramping after the nurse line did not reply.

**[pbu27-0298]**

> I bring up to this lady that I’ve been having some spotting and cramping and the nurse line never called me back. I ask if I can speak to anyone about this, and I QUOTE, she said “we already went over this. If you’re not bleeding through a pad an hour, you’re fine.”

**Request / named next step:** Reports having to ask for dog walking, rubbish and other ordinary household help.

**[pbu27-0301]**

> He cleaned the toilet once the entire month I was sick. Bathtub never got cleaned (and he is blue collar so there was dirt everywhere). Dog barely got walked unless I asked. Trash was always forgotten. Nothing was really ever done unless I asked for it to be done.

**Request / named next step:** Explicit list of questions for doctors and prevention planning after severe postpartum hemorrhage.

**[pbu27-0315]**

> **Has anyone had a severe postpartum hemorrhage like this and gone on to have another baby? Did doctors ever determine what caused it? What was done differently during the next pregnancy/delivery?**
> 
> I’d especially love to hear from OBs, MFMs, nurses, or moms who have experienced something similar. I really need help understanding what questions I should be asking my doctors and what can potentially be done to prevent this from happening again.

**Request / named next step:** Second opinion within the same clinic and possible conflict between doctors.

**[pbu27-0322]**

> I have been a mess and my husband finally asked if I would get a second opinion because Im getting conflicting information. So I called today and have an appointment with another doctor at the same clinic. Has anyone done this? The clinic staff is great and the nurses have always been great which is why I want to stay at this practice. Im just concerned staying at the same place will be a conflict of interests between doctors.

**Request / named next step:** Registry items, brands, overlooked needs and which registry service to use.

**[pbu27-0325]**

> With that being said…. this is the first time I’ve gotten far enough in a pregnancy to start thinking about a registry….. and now I’m panicked because I have no idea what to put on it. 
> 
>   
> Do any of you have recommendations or suggestions for items, brands, and not so obvious needs that I should add to a registry? I’m thinking we’re probably going to use Amazon… but do any of you have any other recommendations for places to make a registry?

**Request / named next step:** Practical help with persistent hunger despite slower eating and snacks.

**[pbu27-0327]**

> This has led me to be starving after I finish up a meal. I have tried eating slower, eating snack in-between meals. Nothing is working. I am not sure if anyone could tell me why this is happening or how to fix it but I need it to stop

**Request / named next step:** Clear choice of calling L&D, waiting or attending after an unexplained gush.

**[pbu27-0332]**

> I put a pad on and it felt relatively dry and I haven’t felt anything else the rest of the day but I’m nervous because I simply cannot tell if it was a weird sweat or discharge mishap or a gush of amniotic fluid. I’m leaning towards it being nothing but like… I can’t be certain I guess? 
> 
> Anyways do I call L&D, wait it out? Just go in? Any advice?

**Request / named next step:** Coping skills while changing medication is unavailable.

**[pbu27-0337]**

> I just want to feel like I’m not alone, I need some coping skills really bad since changing my medicine is pretty much off the table for now. How does anyone get through this? I’m so jealous of mothers who can not worry and everything be okay:( please help!

**Request / named next step:** Whether to try Unisom/B6 before first clinician appointment.

**[pbu27-0342]**

> I’m 8w+2 days and morning sickness is awful 😭 I’ve seen a lot of people talk about Unisom + b6. I haven’t had my first appointment yet and I’m wonder if it’s worth it to try it without speaking to my doctor first?

**Request / named next step:** An at-home coping plan during a month without routine OB care.

**[pbu27-0343]**

> But I won't get another ultrasound or any non-emergency OB care until I am 16 weeks and the disappearing pregnancy symptoms are scaring me. I dont necessarily want to be miserable, but I dont want to find out in November that I lost the baby. The constant debilitating nausea was what kept me going "hey, but at least this means the baby is healthy." And idk how else to tell that everything is okay. 
> 
> I know going to the ER for an ultrasound just so I can see my baby is ridiculous. So how can I, at home, keep myself calm and positive that everything is going to be okay for the next month?

**Request / named next step:** Requested and obtained an OB note for light duty and physical restrictions.

**[pbu27-0356]**

> I have been having issues being provided breaks to use the restroom, being put into rooms for long periods of time sometimes with no lunch or dinner break. So I had talked to my ob and she wrote a note saying I needed to be placed on light duty, I can not lift more than 10 lbs and that I can’t be bending over constantly (I’ve been having a lot of dizzy spells).
